172: INTRODUCTION: CHILD HEALTH IN PAPUA NEW 
175: centres in the media, it is important to understand that Papua New Guinea is still - in 2002 - a 
176: predominantly rural country, with 85% of the population living in rural villages. Furthermore, a 
178: More than 40% of the population is less than 15 years old, and more than 15% less than 5 years. Most 
179: deaths in children - both in rural areas and in urban hospitals - are caused by acute infectious diseases, 
192: Doctors responsible for children in Papua New Guinea have a dual role. Whilst based in urban centres 
194: improve the delivery of health care to rural villages and to disadvantaged urban communities. To do this 
195: effectively, local beliefs and practices must be taken into account (Lancet 2:152-154,1988). Doctors 
200: Doctors caring for children in Papua New Guinea must: 
203: (Tropical Doctor 19:126-130,1989). Doctors must NOT spend all their time treating the lucky few 
204: who are able to come to their hospital. These will consist almost entirely of children who live less 
205: than 16 km away. Think of all the others! 
206: 2. Encourage and supervise Maternal and Child Health (MCH) services in their area. Participate in 
207: regular in-service courses for Child Health Nurses.

223: ## Page 13
229: Lancet 2:1012,1976). 
230: Think about the child\u2019s past and future health 
231: This book concentrates on the immediate management of a child who is ill. A child\u2019s illness, however, is 
232: usually only a brief incident in his or her life. It is important that the doctor who cares for a child during 
233: an episode of illness does not neglect either the child\u2019s past or future. 
234: The past: enquire about past illnesses, the health of the family, the child\u2019s birth history and 
235: developmental progress. Look at the child\u2019s Health Record Book, which most mothers will have with 
236: them. This book provides a valuable record of the child\u2019s past health, weight (growth) record and 
237: immunisation status. It also gives the child\u2019s birth date, so that you can work out his or her exact age. 
238: The future: before discharging a child from hospital, write down in their Health Record Book the reason 
242: Sick children in Papua New Guinea frequently suffer from several diseases at once. Whilst treating the 
243: child\u2019s main illness do not neglect other illnesses, eg malnutrition, anaemia or scabies. Check the child\u2019s 
245: child\u2019s time in hospital to provide the family with some health education. If the mother is pregnant,

372: THE PROBLEMS OF CHILD HEALTH IN PAPUA NEW 
374: 1. Large numbers of children. Approximately two fifths of the population is under the age of l5 years, one third under the age of 10 years and 15% less than 5 years. Less than 2% are over the age 
375: of 60 years. 
377: towns, 85% of the population still lived in rural areas in 2000.

382: 5. Low literacy rate. In spite of improvements in the last few years, only 70% of children of primary 
383: school age and 15% of children of secondary school age attend school. Less girls than boys attend 
388: 30 kina (whilst the amount has increased in the last year, health expenditure per capita in real terms 
389: of purchasing power has fallen since the early 1980s). 
390: 8. Shortage of professional manpower. The doctor-population ratio is l to 15,500. But most of the 
391: doctors live in urban areas; in rural areas the doctor-population ratio is closer to 1 to 50,000. 
398: 11. Young children frequently have more than one disease. 
399: THE INFANT MORTALITY RATE AND UNDER 5 MORTALITY 
401: Overall, the infant mortality rate in the early 2000s approximates 80 per 1,000 live births with an under 
402: five mortality rate of between 100-120 per 1,000 live births. It is higher in remote areas and considerably 
403: lower in urban areas. About half the infant deaths are in babies less than one month old (neonatal 
404: deaths), and the other half are in infants 1-11 months old (post-neonatal deaths). These figures are 
406: A most dramatic drop in infant mortality was documented in the Wam area of the East Sepik Province of 
407: Papua New Guinea, from 350 per 1,000 in 1959 to 75 per 1,000 in 1969. This was brought about by a 
409: and penicillin through the aidpost, the immunisation of infants, antenatal immunisation of mothers with 
410: tetanus toxoid, education of families (about hygiene, sanitation and nutrition) and oral rehydration for 
411: diarrhoea provided through the community health clinics. It is important to stress that it was auxiliary 
413: remarkable fall in infant mortality. Hospital medicine played very little part. 
414: Between 1960 and 1980 the mortality rates in Papua New Guinea dropped substantially. From the mid 
415: 1980s however, they increased, as a result of the breakdown of rural health services. In the middle 1990s 
416: the mortality rates appeared to fall slightly, possibly partly as a result of the Child Survival Programme 
419: ## Page 17
421: launched in 1992, the aim of which was to improve rural health services for children and their mothers. 
423: of 1997 will affect health care and mortality rates, but early signs are not very promising.

473: COMMUNITY HEALTH CLINICS 
474: About 78% of mothers receive some professional antenatal care, and about 53% of mothers receive some 
475: professional help with childbirth - usually in a hospital or health centre. About 90% of children under 
476: one year old are enrolled at community health clinics, many of which are run by the churches. In theory, 
479: areas function intermittently or not at all. Attendance at community health clinics varies widely from 
481: At present, about 90% of young children have received BCG vaccine, but less than two-thirds have 
482: received a third dose of triple antigen or Sabin vaccine, or routine measles vaccine. Less than 40% have

540: TRADITIONAL CHILD-REARING PRACTICES 
541: It is essential that doctors realise that many of the traditional child-rearing practices in Papua New 
543: examples are the care of children in hospital by their families and breast feeding. 
544: Mothers looking after their own children in hospital provides the following advantages: 
545: - Breast feeding can be maintained
548: - Mothers provide a 24 hours per day nurse for their child at no cost
551: This traditional practice of relatives caring for their children in hospital must be encouraged. Never place 
552: young children in \u201cmetal cages\u201d (cots). Let them sleep with their mothers on low beds. 
553: BREAST FEEDING 
560: promote breast feeding and to discourage artificial feeding. In Papua New Guinea, feeding bottles, teats 
561: and infant feeding cups (with perforated spouts) may only be obtained legally on prescription from a 
562: registered pharmacist. The Papua New Guinea Health Department Infant Feeding Policy prohibits the 
563: use of feeding bottles or feeding cups in hospitals and health centres. If a baby has to be artificially fed, 
566: policy in relation to women infected with HIV is that they should be encouraged to breast feed. This is 
567: because the risks of the baby dying from infection and malnutrition as a result of not breast feeding are 
568: considered greater than the relatively small risks of acquiring HIV infection through breast feeding. 
569: Current understanding of the balance of risks is that breast feeding in HIV positive women should 
570: probably continue for around 6 months. For the individual HIV infected woman with an appreciation of 
571: the requirements for artificial feeding and the means to support it, the doctor may well choose to advise 
572: this. Artificial feeding is not, however, synonomous with bottle feeding. Reduction of perinatal

978: ACUTE DISTENSION IN INFANTS 
979: Acute abdominal distension is common in small children with severe sepsis such as pneumonia, 
980: meningitis or septicaemia. Hypokalaemia is common in these children; measure serum potassium if this 
983: Acute abdominal distension requiring surgery is uncommon in infants, but is suggested by bilious 
984: vomiting (very suggestive of obstruction in a neonate), distension with active bowel sounds, or signs of 
988: 1. If intranasal oxygen is being given, make sure the catheter is not pushed in too far (into the 
989: oesophagus) and that the flow rate is not more than 1 litre per minute. 
990: 2. Pass a size 8 nasogastric tube and aspirate. Give nil by mouth. 
991: 3. Give IV fluids. If hypokalaemia is present or suspected, up to 0.4 mEq/kg/hour of KCl (l gram = 
992: 13.3 mEq = 13.3 mmol) can be infused IV in an emergency, but 0.2 mEq/kg/hour is much safer.

1063: The great majority of children with anaemia have iron deficiency anaemia as shown by the hypochromic 
1067: suggestive of iron deficiency in the presence of infection, particularly in a malnourished child. Malaria is 
1068: a major contributor to anaemia. Hookworm may also contribute, especially in children over 2 years old. 
1070: A child with a Hb 5-10 g/dl is usually treated as an outpatient. A child with a Hb under 5 g/dl is usually 
1074: For mild to moderate anaemia (Hb 5-10 g/dl), give the standard 3-day treatment for uncomplicated 
1075: malaria (infant Camoquin/chloroquine daily for 3 days and single dose Fansidar). 
1076: For severe anaemia (Hb <5 g/dl), give the standard treatment for complicated/severe malaria. 
1078: Give prophylactic infant Camoquin or chloroquine weekly for 12 weeks.

1383: RELAXANT ANAESTHESIA IN A CHILD OVER 3 MONTHS OF 
1386: 1. Give no solids for 6 hours preoperatively, and no clear fluids for 4 hours preoperatively. Insert an

2712: BLOOD PRESSURE MEASUREMENT IN INFANTS 
2713: In infants and young children, you should use the widest blood pressure cuff that will fit on the upper 
2714: arm. A cuff that covers the whole upper arm is more accurate in children than one that covers only two 
2715: thirds of the upper arm (J Pediatr 92:934-938,1978). If you do not have a cuff of the correct size you 
2717: Pump up the cuff to 120 mmHg. This is usually enough, and higher pressures just disturb the child. 
2725: 3. Use the flush method. Firmly compress the hand and forearm in one hand to squeeze all the blood 
2726: out, THEN pump up the cuff to 120 mmHg while still squeezing. Stop squeezing, then allow the 
2727: mercury to fall 5 mm at a time, waiting for 5 seconds at each step. The end-point is when the limb 
2728: suddenly goes pink, and is about 5 mmHg less than the true systolic blood pressure. Repeat the 
2733: 0-3 years

3956: Hypoglycaemia is common in children with severe infections. It is particularly common in malnourished 
3957: children. Always check a comatose child for hypoglycaemia with a dextrostix if you can. If you cannot, 
3958: it is always reasonable to treat for hypoglycaemia with intravenous 10% dextrose (5 ml/kg) or 50% 
3959: dextrose (1 ml/kg). Hypoglycaemia is a recognised complication of parenteral quinine treatment.

4865: associated with malnutrition) is seen occasionally. Type 2 (non-insulin-dependent) diabetes may also be

4892: of normal saline and given at 0.1 ml/kg/hr (equating to 0.1 U/kg/hr of insulin) in a side drip. If an 
4893: infusion cannot be loaded quickly, commence therapy with 0.1 ml/kg given SC or IM every hour.

4936: Diarrhoea is one of the top four causes of death and morbidity in Papua New Guinean children. 
4940: and are preventable by oral rehydration therapy (ORT) or if necessary intravenous rehydration 
4944: 3. Persistent diarrhoea. Diarrhoea which persists for more than 14 days. (In PNG 7 days is taken as 
4946: (up to 20-30%). It is almost invariably associated with severe malnutrition and affected children 
4949: The main cause of death in diarrhoeal disease of children is dehydration. Emphasis must therefore 
4953: naturally done - fluids are often withheld if a child has diarrhoea. In discussing the management of her 
4954: child with a mother it can be useful to use the example of a child being like a plastic bag containing 
4955: water. If the bag gets a leak (if the child gets diarrhoea) it goes slack (like dehydration) and more water 
4956: must be put in to keep it full. In the case of the child, the leak usually stops after 2 to 3 days. 
4957: Most \u201chome\u201d fluids are suitable to give in the early stages of diarrhoea, before dehydration occurs. 
4958: Coconut water, tea, mueli water (lemon water), soups from cooking vegetables, \u201cGuava water\u201d (water 
4962: Once dehydration occurs it is necessary to use Oral Rehydration Solution - either the Unicef product - 
4963: ORS - (1 sachet dissolved in 1 litre of water) or a home made solution made of a heaped teaspoon of 
4967: severely dehydrated children can be satisfactorily rehydrated with ORS. \u201cCereal based\u201d Oral 
4970: vegetables and containing a little salt (the amount used for normal cooking) is an efficient form of ORS. 
4972: Guinea the policy is in general to reserve ORS for treatment of dehydrated children in health centre or 
4974: The step by step management of children with diarrhoea is detailed in the Paediatric Standard Treatment 
4978: ## Page 115
4981: 1. Always encourage mother to continue breastfeeding (unless there is evidence of severe lactose 
4983: 2. Give food to children with diarrhoea - they require more calories, not less. 
4987: Treat the child as an outpatient. Discuss with the mother the importance of giving extra fluids every time 
4988: the child has a watery stool and give some fluids to the child to drink under supervision. 
4990: Encourage mother to continue breastfeeding and to give extra food. 
4991: Encourage the mother to bring the child back for reassessment if the diarrhoea becomes worse, becomes 
4992: bloodstained or continues for more than two days, if there is persistent vomiting, if the child becomes 
4993: very thirsty or loses interest in feeding and drinking or if there is persistent fever. 
4995: Signs of dehydration start to appear when the child is 4-5% dehydrated (4-5% of body weight). Signs of 
4996: severe dehydration and shock appear when the child is 10% dehydrated or more. 
4998: If the child has signs of dehydration but is not shocked, is not vomiting frequently and does not have a 
5000: child. The child should be given as much as he or she can drink - at least a big cup every hour. 
5001: Note: It is better to give small sips very frequently than to try and force the child to drink a large amount 
5002: all at once if the child is unwilling to do so, or if he vomits after a large amount. One teaspoon (5 ml) 
5003: every minute is 300 ml in an hour - a substantial amount! 
5005: If the child has signs of severe dehydration, shock, refuses to drink, is vomiting repeatedly or has a 
5007: fluid is half strength Darrow\u2019s solution. This fluid contains Na 61, K 18, Cl 52, and HCO3 27 mmol/l in 
5008: 2.5% dextrose. It is specifically designed for treatment of diarrhoea and is suitable for the initial 
5009: management of all forms of severe dehydration, hypo-, iso- and hypertonic. It is given in steps of 20 ml/kg as fast as possible with regular reassessment until signs of dehydration improve, and then is 
5010: maintained at a rate of about 150 ml/kg per 24 hours. 
5011: IVT can be discontinued when the child is drinking satisfactorily. 
5012: If you are unable to put in an intravenous line, give fluids by an alternative route. 
5014: 1. Nasogastric. This can be used if the abdomen is not distended, the child is not vomiting repeatedly 
5015: and is not shocked. Make sure the tube is in the stomach, and splint the child\u2019s arms so that he 
5016: cannot dislodge it. ORS or half strength Darrow\u2019s are ideal. 20 mls/kg can be given every hour. 
5018: 3. Intraperitoneal. This cannot be used if there is abdominal distension, or if the child is profoundly

5742: HEATERS FOR NEONATES IN COLD AREAS

6175: FEMORAL VEIN PUNCTURE 
6176: This should only be performed in children over 10 kg. Do not do a femoral puncture on a neonate. If 
6177: you have difficulty obtaining blood in a small child, do a radial arterial puncture (p.34). 
6178: 1. Have an assistant hold the child supine with the right hip extended and externally rotated. 
6179: 2. Clean the skin with 70% alcohol (SVM). 
6180: 3. Palpate the femoral artery just below the midpoint of a line joining the anterior superior iliac spine 
6182: 4. While still palpating the artery with your left index finger, push a 21 gauge needle on a 5 ml or 10 ml 
6183: syringe through the skin at a very shallow angle at a point 2 cm below the inguinal crease. The vein 
6184: is very close to the skin, unless the child is very fat. Aspirate gently and withdraw the needle slowly 
6187: 5. After obtaining the blood and withdrawing the needle, have an assistant press firmly on the puncture 
6188: site with a dry swab for 2 minutes. 
6189: Do NOT do a femoral puncture in a patient with nephrotic syndrome.

7093: and will become one of the most important causes of immunodeficiency in children in PNG. 
7095: The first case of HIV infection in PNG was identified in June 1987 and the first clinical case of acquired 
7096: immune deficiency syndrome (AIDS) was reported to the Department of Health in March 1988. Since 
7097: then the numbers have been increasing rapidly. As of December 2001, more than 5,000 HIV seropositive 
7098: persons had been reported to the Health Department, including some 100 children, the majority of whom 
7099: were below 18 months of age. 
7105: For infants and children, the most common mode of transmission is from mother to child - vertical 
7107: Modes of transmission of HIV in infants and children

7117: vertical transmission rates can only be obtained from prospective studies of cohorts of children born to 
7119: range from 14% to 39% in different studies.

7124: high, the substantial protective benefits of breastfeeding outweigh the possible risk of transmission in 
7125: most cases. Breastfeeding should therefore be encouraged in the majority of cases (see p.154).

7162: Guidelines for recognising HIV infection in children 
7163: A diagnosis of HIV infection in children is made if the following are present: 
7175: oral thrush, failure to thrive, skin rashes, fever longer than 1 month, diarrhoea longer than 14 days, generalised lymphadenopathy 
7179: \xbe Two associated findings and laboratory evidence of HIV infection in the child. 
7185: ## Page 164
7190: Paediatric AIDS is suspected in a child presenting with at least 2 major signs associated 
7191: with 2 minor signs in the absence of known causes of immunosuppression, such as cancer, 
7194: - Chronic diarrhoea of more than 1 month duration
7195: - Prolonged fever of more than 1 month duration
7200: - Persistent cough for more than 1 month
7206: positive predictive value. In PNG most children with tuberculosis would fulfil the criteria. 
7207: LABORATORY DIAGNOSIS 
7209: for the actual presence of the virus or viral antigens. As IgG antibodies cross the placenta, all children 
7211: they may just be carrying maternal antibodies. These antibodies disappear over the first 18 months of 
7212: life if the child is not infected, but they persist beyond that age if the child is infected with HIV. So in 
7213: PNG a definite diagnosis of HIV infection can only be made when the child is 18 months old. 
7214: Elsewhere, assays which test for the actual presence of virus, such as p24 antigen tests, virus culture and 
7216: negative and generally 2 consecutive positive tests are required to decide that a baby is HIV infected.

7915: - breastfeeding is a form of community family spacing.

7919: should be made to discourage artificial feeding. In particular, every effort should be made to discourage

8051: Give babies as much as they want as often as they want. Most babies take 5-6 feeds every day. 
8052: A guide to how much milk to give with each feed is to multiply the baby\u2019s weight in kg by 30 (maximum 240 ml per 
8053: feed). A baby weighing 5 kg would be offered 5 x 30 = 150 ml each feed 5 - 6 times a day. 
8054: As the baby takes as much milk per feed as he or she wants, there should always be a little milk left over after each 
8055: feed. So if the baby finished all the feed, offer a little more next time.

8536: Leprosy is quite common in children, but rare under 2 years of age. It is often unrecognised, and 75% of 
8537: cases regress spontaneously without treatment. Childhood leprosy usually responds rapidly to treatment 
8538: and reactions are rare. Almost all cases in children are indeterminate or tuberculoid leprosy. 
8539: Leprosy bacilli are intracellular organisms. Host resistance therefore depends on cellular immunity and 
8541: contact with a patient is NOT needed to contract leprosy. The incubation period is usually about 3 years 
8542: (range 3 months to 40 years). 
8546: sensory loss of 2 or 3 modalities or superficial nerve enlargement). 
8548: develops. Usually cellular immunity destroys all the organisms and the patch disappears spontaneously. 
8554: Tuberculoid leprosy occurs when cellular immunity is strong, but not strong enough to kill off all the

8646: The treatment of leprosy was reviewed in Lancet 2:487,1988. Most children with leprosy have 
8647: paucibacillary disease. They can be treated for just 6 months with daily unsupervised dapsone and 
8648: monthly supervised rifampicin 15 mg/kg (maximum 600 mg). All treatment then stops and the patient 
8649: remains under observation for 2 years.

8653: observation should continue for 5 years.

9876: ## Page 221
9879: Malnutrition is common in Papua New Guinea. Around one in three children in some parts of the 
9884: mortality of children in Papua New Guinea. (The effects of malnutrition on intellect are discussed in 
9885: Grantham-McGregor SM. The effect of malnutrition on mental development. In: Waterlow JC. Protein 
9889: published that correlate mortality with age, height and weight in children in developing countries: 
9890: Lancet 1:1247-1250,1978 (weight-for-age only) and Am J Clin Nutr 33:1836-45,1980. These and other 
9892: nutritional indices are discussed in Malnutrition and mortality, chap 18. In: Waterlow JC. Protein energy 
9896: The standard method of assessing nutritional status is to express the weight of the child as a percentage 
9898: median weight (50th percentile) for American children of that age (Harvard Standard Weight), and this is 
9899: called the 100% weight-for-age of children in Papua New Guinea and other \u201cthird world\u201d countries. 
9900: Children over 80% of their expected weight-for-age are said to be well nourished. The 80% line on the 
9901: Papua New Guinea weight-for-age chart approximates the 3rd centile of the Harvard Standard Weight. 
9902: Children 60% to 80% of the expected weight have moderate malnutrition, and those under 60% have 
9903: severe malnutrition (marasmus). One study in India showed that, on average, child mortality doubled 
9904: with each 10% decline below 80% of the Harvard Standard Weight (Lancet 1:1247-1250,1978). But in 
9905: Bangladeshi children, mortality did not increase until weight-for-age was less than 70% of the Harvard 
9906: Standard Weight (Am J Clin Nutr 33:1836,1980). The presence of oedema due to malnutrition indicates 
9908: The pattern of growth should be considered as well as weight-for-age. A child with a weight that is static 
9909: or falling for three months or more has malnutrition, even if his or her weight-for-age is over 80%. A 
9910: child who is steadily gaining weight and who remains at a constant percentage weight-for-age probably 
9913: weight or change in weight-for-age (Br Med J 298:1607-11,1989). 
9915: chart in a child\u2019s health record book provides an invaluable tool for following the pattern of growth. The 
9916: book is essential to proper childcare and every effort should be made to see that all children have a book 
9919: Weight-for-age is not an ideal measurement of nutritional status. The age of many children is not known, 
9920: and counting the number of teeth present then adding six to get the age in months (J Pediatr 64:97,1964) 
9921: gives only an approximate estimate of age . Children are often weighed inaccurately, and the weight 
9923: reasonable assessment of nutritional status in children one to five years of age (Lancet 1:758,1974 and 
9924: Lancet 1:87-89,1987). During this period, the MUAC stays relatively constant, and children with a 
9925: circumference of less than 14 cm are considered to be malnourished (In Papua New Guinea, 13.5 cm is 
9928: ## Page 222
9932: (see Lancet 2:1337-1338,1985 and Lancet 2:725-728,1987). 
9934: Children who are below 80% of the Harvard Standard Weight may be too light because they are very 
9937: while wasting suggests recent severe malnutrition. To decide if an underweight child is stunted or 
9938: wasted, height has to be measured in addition to age and weight. In Bangladeshi children, weight-for-age 
9940: weakest index of mortality (Am J Clin Nutr 33:1836-1845,1980). Similar results have been obtained in 
9941: children in Papua New Guinea. 
9943: What can be done to improve nutrition (see Lancet 1:334-336,1988)? Supplementary feeding 
9944: programmes are expensive, often impracticable, and of questionable benefit (Nutrition Reviews 9:278280,1978). Control of intestinal parasites may be useful in selected groups (Lancet 2:108-110,1977). 
9945: Maternal malnutrition causes low birthweight babies (Am J Clin Nutr 28:1223,1975) that fail to thrive 
9946: (Lancet 2:178,1978). Perhaps more emphasis should be put on improving the nutrition of pregnant and 
9950: infant origins of adult disease. BMJ publications 1992). Thus, the benefits of improving antenatal health 
9953: (Lancet 2:487-488,1987). 
9955: The short- term effects of nutrition education should not be overestimated. Salfield (PNG Med J 17:179182,1974) showed little change in the nutritional status of a rural community after a year of intensive 
9956: nutrition education. Care must be taken not to blame malnutrition on the \u201cignorance\u201d of mothers: if 30% 
9957: of children are malnourished, then 70% are well fed. Traditional feeding practices may not be perfect, 
9959: soundly based (see Trop Doctor 6:37-42,1976). 
9960: Attempts to improve the nutritional status of a community will be most effective if there is close cooperation between the departments of health, agriculture and education, and involve international 
9962: nutritionist. The main emphasis ought to be on education of the community, rather than individual 
9971: Highlands, see PNG Med J 22:65-71,1979.

11118: NEONATES - APNOEA 
11119: Neonates often have periodic breathing, with spells of rapid breathing for about 20 seconds alternating 
11120: with cessation of breathing for about 10 seconds. Apnoea is said to occur if the baby stops breathing for 
11121: more than 20 seconds (though symptoms may occur before this). The baby may develop bradycardia, 
11123: respiratory distress. Attacks may occur frequently for 2 weeks or more before gradually becoming less

### Bubble-CPAP-guidelines-2017
1: # Bubble-CPAP Guidelines (2017)
5: | Source | `Bubble-CPAP-guidelines-2017.pdf` |
9: ## Page 1
11: # Guidelines for Use of Bubble-CPAP Concentrators
13: ## Page 2
15: Standard oxygen therapy, either using concentrators or cylinders, saves the lives of many children. Use CPAP if the child is failing standard oxygen therapy.
17: ### Indications for CPAP
19: - Severe respiratory distress despite oxygen therapy
20: - Severe hypoxaemia (SpO\u2082 <85%) despite high-flow oxygen therapy
22: CPAP is usually effective for children with pneumonia and bronchiolitis. If children have other comorbidities, such as anaemia, TB, or meningitis, these need to be treated also, or CPAP alone will not be useful.
24: ### Conditions for which CPAP is not usually useful or is contraindicated
26: CPAP is contraindicated if a child has pneumothorax, as it can make the air leak worse. In some conditions, such as staphylococcal pneumonia with pneumatocoeles on chest X-ray, CPAP can increase the risk of pneumothorax, so caution is needed.
28: CPAP will not be effective in an unconscious child who is not taking breaths of their own.
30: CPAP is not usually useful for children with congenital heart disease with pulmonary hypertension, or severe chronic lung disease with pulmonary hypertension.
32: ### Checklist if a child is hypoxaemic despite oxygen
34: Before using CPAP in a child on oxygen, check:
36: 1. Oxygen is flowing (put the end of the tube under water in a beaker and watch for bubbles, or hold the end close to your hand to feel the air flow).
37: 2. The oxygen tubing is not leaking.
39: 4. If oxygen is delivered from a concentrator, the concentration delivered is adequate (>85%) using an oxygen analyser.
44: 9. For ventilatory failure: the child's respiratory effort is inadequate, or the child has slow or shallow breathing and is lethargic.
45: 10. Increase oxygen through nasal prongs to maximum flow (4 L/min for infants and up to 8 L/min for older children).
47: If the child is still hypoxaemic or has severe respiratory distress, it is appropriate to use CPAP.
49: ## Page 3
51: ### What is CPAP?
53: Continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) consists of delivery of mild air pressure to keep the airways open. CPAP delivers positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) with a variable amount of oxygen to the airway of a spontaneously breathing patient to maintain lung volume during expiration. CPAP decreases atelectasis (alveolar and lung segmental collapse) and respiratory fatigue, and improves oxygenation. It is indicated for infants with severe respiratory distress, hypoxaemia, or apnoea despite receiving oxygen.
55: CPAP requires a source of continuous airflow (often an air compressor) and usually requires an oxygen blender connected to an oxygen source. A CPAP system should be used only when it is reliable, when oxygen systems are in place, where staff are adequately trained, and when close monitoring is assured.
57: Any bubble CPAP system has three main components:
59: 1. **Continuous oxygen and air flow into the circuit:** The gas flow rate required to generate CPAP is usually 5\u201310 L/min. All children with severe pneumonia and many neonates with RDS require supplemental oxygen. Therefore, the system requires an oxygen blender, which connects an oxygen source (cylinder or concentrator) to the continuous airflow to increase the FiO\u2082.
60: 2. **A nasal interface connecting the infant's airway with the circuit:** Short nasal prongs are generally used to deliver nasal CPAP. They must be carefully fitted to minimize leakage of air (otherwise CPAP will not be achieved) and to reduce nasal trauma.
61: 3. **An expiratory limb with the distal end submerged in water to generate end-expiratory pressure:** In bubble CPAP, the positive pressure is maintained by placing the far end of the expiratory tubing in water. The pressure is adjusted by altering the depth of the tube under the surface of the water.
63: ## Page 4
65: A gas (oxygen) flow rate of 5\u201310 L/min is required for older children with pneumonia, while 3\u20134 L/min may be sufficient to generate CPAP in small neonates. In premature neonates <32 weeks' gestation, pure oxygen is not safe, as a high concentration can cause retinopathy of prematurity.
67: ### The CPAP concentrator
69: Oxygen concentrators are machines that extract nitrogen from atmospheric air, resulting in an output of almost pure oxygen. CPAP is continuous positive airway pressure, which can assist children with severe respiratory distress. The bubble-CPAP concentrator delivers both oxygen and continuous positive airway pressure; the level depends on how far the tubing is submerged in the water in the bottle.
76: - Bottle with dial-up CPAP level
78: - Oxygen flow meter
80: - Oxygen/air mixing chart
83: ### Setting up the bubble-CPAP concentrator
87: ## Page 5
89: 1. Position the concentrator at least 30 cm away from walls or curtains so the inlet opening at the back is not obstructed.
91: 3. Turn on the concentrator (switch on the console). Always have the oxygen flow dial turned on when you turn the concentrator on; this prevents a build-up of pressure inside the concentrator. Gas should flow immediately from the gas outlet. An orange light will go off when sufficient oxygen concentration is reached, usually within 10 minutes.
95: 7. Dial up the level of CPAP required: start at 7 cmH\u2082O.
96: 8. Connect the nasal prongs to the child as with normal oxygen prongs.
97: 9. Dial the flows of air and oxygen required to see a constant stream of bubbles in the water. Start with 5 L/min of oxygen and 5 L/min of air for a 5 kg child, and 3 L/min of oxygen and 3 L/min of air for a neonate. Check and record the flows dialled and the oxygen concentration this provides. A total flow of 10 L/min is sufficient in most cases to deliver CPAP, visible as continuous bubbling in the bottle. In smaller infants and neonates, a much lower flow will often still deliver CPAP; a total flow of 5 L/min is often enough.
99: **Note:** The bottle CPAP level is often dialled at 3 cmH\u2082O initially, but 5\u20137 cmH\u2082O is usually needed.
101: ## Page 6
103: 10. Use the lowest total flow that provides bubbling (depends on patient size, CPAP level required, and nasal leak). Usually a flow of about 2 L per kg per min is maximal (e.g. for a 6 kg child, a total maximal flow of 12 L/min).
104: 11. Check the child for signs of respiratory distress, check SpO\u2082, and check for bubbles in the bottle.
105:   - If SpO\u2082 is below 90% or the child has severe respiratory distress, first increase the CPAP level to 8 or 10 cmH\u2082O and then, if no response, increase the oxygen flow meter to as much as 8 L/min.
107:   - If there are still not continuous bubbles, check for leaks along the circuit and adjust the oxygen or air flows according to the chart below.
111: | SpO\u2082 >90%, mild respiratory distress | Yes | No immediate change needed; may reduce CPAP level to 5 cmH\u2082O or reduce flow rates |
112: | SpO\u2082 >90%, moderate to severe respiratory distress | Yes | Increase CPAP level |
113: | SpO\u2082 <90% | Yes | Increase CPAP level; increase oxygen flow |
114: | Any status | No | Check nasal prongs and circuit for leaks; increase air or oxygen flow and check for bubbles |
116: ## Page 7
125: | 4 | Feeding | Normal | Difficulty with feeding or reduced feeding because of respiratory distress | Unable to feed because of respiratory distress or lethargy |
129: ### Trials off CPAP and when to stop CPAP
131: When children who are clinically stable (low respiratory distress score and SpO\u2082 >92%), CPAP should be disconnected for 10\u201315 minutes, the child should be put on standard-flow oxygen, and clinical signs and SpO\u2082 should be carefully examined to assess whether CPAP is still required.
133: Trials off CPAP are best done first thing in the morning, when there is likely to be adequate staff to observe the child throughout the day. If trials off CPAP are started in the late afternoon, low staff numbers overnight and the oxygen desaturation that sometimes occurs during sleep mean there is a risk of hypoxaemia developing unrecognized overnight.
135: Children who have SpO\u2082 <90% or a high RDS (>10) while still on CPAP, or who are unstable or very unwell, should not be given trials on room air.
137: Some children will become hypoxaemic rapidly when taken off CPAP; this is a marker of very severe disease. Increase their oxygen or put them back on CPAP immediately. You should be at the bedside, monitor SpO\u2082, and watch the child for cyanosis or severe respiratory distress. Instruct parents and nursing staff on what to observe.
139: ## Page 8
141: ### Steps in CPAP weaning
143: If the child on CPAP is clinically stable (low respiratory distress score and SpO\u2082 >92%), disconnect from CPAP as described below for 10\u201315 minutes and carefully examine for changes in clinical signs and SpO\u2082 to assess whether CPAP is still required:
145: 1. Wean the bubble CPAP level to off, wean air flow to off, and reduce concentrator oxygen flow to 2 L/min (do not turn oxygen to 0, as the concentrator needs some gas flowing whenever it is running).
146: 2. Check the child after 15\u201330 minutes and record the respiratory score. If RDS <10 and SpO\u2082 >95%, wean off oxygen and turn off the concentrator.
147: 3. Check at 15 min, 30 min, and 1 hr.
148: 4. If SpO\u2082 >95% on room air, stay off CPAP and off oxygen.
149: 5. If SpO\u2082 90\u201395% and RDS <7: put on standard oxygen using wall or cylinder oxygen and normal nasal oxygen prongs.
150: 6. If SpO\u2082 <90% or RDS >10, put back on CPAP: neonates \u2014 bubbling CPAP level at 5 cmH\u2082O, oxygen 5 L/min, air 5 L/min; children >1 year \u2014 start CPAP level at 7 cmH\u2082O, oxygen 7 L/min, air 7 L/min. Dial up if not bubbling or saturation is not steady or the child desaturates.
151: 7. Trial off CPAP again in 24 hours if the child is stable.
154: **Children with acute respiratory disease should not be discharged until:**
156: 1. SpO\u2082 has been stable at >90% while breathing room air for at least 24 hours.
159: 4. The parents or guardian understand the danger signs to look for, when to return if the child becomes sicker, and when to return for a planned review.
161: ## Page 9
165: This is essential to reduce the risk of cross-infection. Cleaning should be done after every patient has used the CPAP, and weekly if the same child is on CPAP for over a week. A spare circuit is essential to replace the one being cleaned without interruption to the child's treatment.
167: The CPAP circuit (inspiratory and expiratory limb, bottle, lid, and connections) must be thoroughly cleaned as follows:
179:   - Disinfectant solution: sodium hypochlorite 0.05% or household bleach diluted to 0.05% hypochlorite (the bleach bottle will indicate its strength; dilution is essential)
188: 2. Prepare detergent (soapy water) and disinfectant solution while wearing PPE: gown or impermeable apron as a minimum, eye protection, and surgical mask for bleach solution.
190: ## Page 10
199: ### Routine cleaning and maintenance of the CPAP concentrator
201: Each week the oxygen concentrator will require approximately 30 minutes of attention. Concentrators have a large particulate filter over the air inlet opening (usually at the back of freestanding or portable models). This filter stops dust and other airborne particles from entering the unit. The filter should be removed and cleaned in warm soapy water, completely dried with an absorbent towel, and replaced. Have a spare dry filter available so there are minimal interruptions to concentrator function.
203: The exterior of the oxygen concentrator should be cleaned with a mild disinfecting cleaning agent or a diluted solution of bleach (usually 5.25% sodium hypochlorite). A solution in the range of 1:100 to 1:10 bleach to water can be used effectively. Allow the solution to remain on the surface for 10 minutes, then rinse off and dry.

13: | Weight (kg) | Every 2 hours (12 feeds) | Every 3 hours (8 feeds) | Every 4 hours (6 feeds) | Daily total (130 ml/kg) | 80% of daily total (minimum) |
59: - Volumes in these columns are rounded to the nearest 5 ml.
60: - Feed 2-hourly for at least the first day. Then, when little or no vomiting, modest diarrhoea (<5 watery stools per day), and finishing most feeds, change to 3-hourly feeds.
61: - After a day on 3-hourly feeds: If no vomiting, less diarrhoea, and finishing most feeds, change to 4-hourly feeds.

120: - After a day on 3-hourly feeds: If no vomiting, less diarrhoea, and finishing most feeds, change to 4-hourly feeds.

### Malnutrition Treatment Chart
9: ## Page 1
25: - Start milk feeding immediately
27:   - At least 6 feeds per day, every 3 hours
28:   - 130 ml/kg/day
29:   - An 8 kg child should receive 8 \xd7 130 = 1040 ml per day; at 6 feeds \u2248 170 ml per feed
30:   - Continue breast feeding
31: - Start iron in the 2nd week of treatment*
33:   - Give Milk Oil Formula (or F-100); increase volume per feed as tolerated
35:   - Continued breast-feeding
38:   - Weigh every 2nd day
39:   - Good weight gain = 10 g/kg/day
42:   - Good weight gain consistently for 1\u20132 weeks, weight >3 Z-scores
44:   - Parents able to feed child
45: - Follow-up weekly
47: For details, refer to Chapter 7, pp. 197\u2013223.
